(8) **Exhaustiveness is quadratic in arms** (about 60% of a debug `check` on 3,000 arms): a small perf item (Opus; `sakura_types` pattern checking): usefulness over a literal column indexes the literals seen instead of re-specialising the whole matrix per row; acceptance: `check` time per arm constant (the 3,000-arm `Int` match and the 3,000-constructor enumeration at most 12× the 250-arm time in a release build), results unchanged on every `tests/ui` pattern fixture, and the 3,000-arm program joins `bench compile` as `check_wide_match` (reported, no budget). Owner and order: (2)–(5) are **M4 items** (they are §17.193 (3)'s own acceptance, `run` with the expected output, which Node cannot give today): Opus, two commits — JS and Core first (`matchc.rs`, `ir.rs`, `sakura_core_snapshot/src/lib.rs`, `emit.rs`, `deep_ir.rs` running the four shapes on Node, JS target), then the LIR and WASM (`sakura_lir` `lir.rs`, `lower.rs`, the validator, `codegen_wasm/src/func.rs`, `deep_ir.rs` on the WASM target). Acceptance: the four generated shapes plus the guarded and a sparse-literal `match` run on Node on both backends with the expected output; `--dump core` of the literal fixtures shows one `Switch`; semantics fixtures pin first-match with wildcard rows between literal rows under both ABIs and on WASM; a function of 257 blocks and one of 256 pin the dispatch forms; `bench compile` within noise; emitted JS of the semantics corpus changes only where an `else` or a join form changed, reviewed as such. Why: an output language has a nesting limit the IR does not, so the IR's guard was necessary and not sufficient; one `Switch` is the shape the source wrote; a two-level table costs one extra `br_table` only in functions no benchmark has. Rejected: a relooper now (a structured region per loop is a performance item, §17.118's lattice; the dispatch stays); a limit on arms; lowering literal switches to a hash lookup (a jump table by arithmetic is one instruction). Performance: `switch` and the dense table are faster than the chain they replace; measured on `tokenizer` and `json_validate` as advisory. Changed: §13.2 (`SwitchOn::Lit`, the output rule), §17.193 (pointer), plan `m3-completion-and-m4.md` §3.8 (row 11 done, rows 16–18). Impact: Opus; Sol none; the owner none.

201. **WS-P11b closes as measured; `deltablue` is representation-bound and joins §17.152's carries; the next performance task is an ablation that sizes ownership-through-parameters before anyone builds it (WS-P11 `71625ab7`, `d2e0fc3d`; plan `performance-r1.md` §8 WS-P11b item 5; round 18).** Facts: WS-P11a's tag compare gave `deltablue` −9%/−12% on Node and −5%/−2.5% on Bun (advisory); WS-P11b's per-map `Int` key kind measured +2–5% on Node and up to +6% on `map_set:string` on Bun and was not landed; the profile after WS-P11a puts 36% in `hget` (the HAMT walk, one `instanceof` per node), 13% in `setAt` (path copies) and 5–6% in GC, with no `eq` or `hashOf` frame; the program still runs about 61× the hand JS. The cause is structural: the Sakura port threads a `Planner { vars: Map<Int, Var>, cons: Map<Int, Con> }` through every function as a parameter record, so §17.149's facts stop at `origin` (a map projected from a parameter is never owned), every `set_value` and `set_mark` path-copies a HAMT node, and every `var_of` walks a trie where the control writes `v.value`. Decision: (1) **WS-P11b is closed as measured**; the key-kind field is not kept (it adds a field and a branch to every map operation for nothing). (2) **`deltablue` is the fourth representation-bound program** under §17.152 (2): the performance round's JS acceptance (plan §5 item 1) reads "no program other than `sieve`, `nbody`, `spectral_norm` and `deltablue`", it is reported with its post-landing ratio and this cause in `bench/deltas.md`, and it is decided at the §17.177 windows as question (6): ownership through parameters, record fields and tuple results (N3 and N4's facts) together with a dense `Int`-keyed map or flat sequence (question (1)); default: the HAMT and the trie stay and the carry is named again. (3) **The next performance task is two steps.** **WS-P13a, the ablation** (Astra, now, no compiler code; a `research/10-evidence` note): measure `deltablue` at n = 1000 and 5000 on Node and Bun with (i) the emitted module hand-edited so the Planner's `Map.insert` calls take `map_insert_owned` (what ownership inference alone could buy; `audit_owned`-style tagging is not needed for a one-off), and (ii) (i) plus `vars`/`cons` replaced by a flat JS array indexed by id (what the representation would buy on top); one table, the same interleaved fresh-process A/B WS-P11 used. **WS-P13b, ownership through parameters, record fields and tuple results** (Opus; `sakura_core/src/opt/owned.rs` with the new `shared` reasons `param`, `field` and `tuple`; `--dump owned`; the LIR reads the same facts; no runtime change, the `*_owned` entries exist) **starts only if (i) shows at least 25% on Node at n = 5000**: a def's parameter is owned when every call site in the module passes an owned value at its last use (bottom-up over the call graph, recursion widened to shared, as §17.149's consuming summaries are computed); a field projection is owned when the record is owned, dead after, or consumed by a record update that replaces that field; a tuple component is owned when the tuple is a fresh allocation of owned parts. Acceptance: `--dump owned` shows `put_var`, `put_con`, `set_value` and `set_mark`'s inserts `owned`; `bench_counters`' `deltablue` dups and drops fall from 277,706 and 269,773 to stated numbers; the advisory delta is within 5 points of (i); fixtures with trace, `--no-direct` and WASM parity: a retained `Planner` version intact after an in-place update, a record captured by a closure, a tuple-threaded owner, a recursive threading widened to shared, the same loop run twice; `audit_owned` clean on the corpus and the differential run; the CODEOWNERS review §17.149 named covers it. If (i) is under 25%, nothing is built and `deltablue` waits for the windows. (4) WS-P11a's delta stands as advisory and is read at the quiet window. Why: the owner's standing goal is speed by default, and the honest path to it here is a number before a mechanism; the ownership proof is the one N3 and N4 need, so building it early is not waste if the number says so; a representation change stays with §17.177 on every backend. Rejected: a mutable or transient map (§17.117, §17.126); a dense-map representation on JS now (§17.152: no representation change before the windows); building WS-P13b on the profile alone (WS-P11b was built on the profile alone and measured flat). Performance: this entry is the measured item; nothing lands without its A/B. Changed: plan `performance-r1.md` §5 item 1, §9 (WS-P11b closed; rows WS-P13a, WS-P13b; order after round 18), §17.152 (pointer), §17.177 (question (6)), plan `m5-native.md` §3. Impact: Astra (WS-P13a), Opus (WS-P13b, conditional), the owner (the windows). **Round 22: WS-P13a measured 22.7% on Node at n = 5000, below the bar; §17.217 names `List` as the mechanism and WS-P13c measures the `List` port before WS-P13b is re-read.** **Round 26: WS-P13b is built; its two deviations are ratified and its conditions set in §17.235.**

202. **§17.194's readings ratified, one corrected: scheme parameters are numbered in sorted order, the snapshot keeps its shape, the order test runs any history, and emitted bytes need their own build-twice test because the emitters hold hash maps (commits `96b8d5d3`, `7f6ec255`, `31916445`; round 18).** (1) **Sorted, not "scheme order".** §17.194 (2)'s "the def's scheme parameters first, in scheme order" is read as built: sorted by `ParamId`, which within one def is creation order and is invariant under the reused interner's base offset, and is the order `runtime_typed_params` and the emitters' `schema{i}` dictionaries index, so the three agree by construction. (2) **The snapshot format keeps its shape** (`sakura-core-snapshot-v1`, §17.107): `FnDef.ty_params` and `row_params` are the validator's bound and do not enter the snapshot. No CLI command reads a snapshot from disk (only tests round-trip one), so there is no trust boundary at which a consumer must check the bound; `Module::check` keeps its unbound-tail rule. The two counts join the format the day a consumer outside the process reads snapshots (a playground, a proof artifact), with the corpus regenerated then. (3) **The order test's first pass runs concurrently and the second in reverse with a primer** is endorsed: it is the stronger statement (any history gives the same bytes), and 37 s of sequential path order bought nothing. (4) **Not ratified as argued: "WASM needs no byte comparison because it is built from the byte-identical snapshot."** The LIR and the WASM emitter lower live Core, not the snapshot (`sakura_lir::from_core`), and `sakura_lir`, `sakura_codegen_wasm` and `sakura_codegen_js` hold about 55 `HashMap`/`HashSet`s between them, whose iteration order is per-instance random, so equal Core does not imply equal bytes. Rule (§13.2): the emitted JS text, the LIR print and the WASM module are functions of Core. Test (Opus, small; `sakura_lir/tests/order.rs`): the semantics corpus built twice in one process and across the two histories compares the WASM bytes and the LIR print as it compares JS; any hash-map iteration that reaches output is replaced by a `BTreeMap` or a sorted walk, found by that test rather than by audit. (5) `31916445`'s percent-decoded Unix paths outside the working directory (`/other/a b.sak`) are §17.196 (3)'s intent, ratified. Why: a sort that is shift-invariant is a canonical order; a bound nobody can reach from outside needs no serialised copy; the determinism rule is testable only where the bytes are produced. Changed: §13.2 (the rule), §17.194 (pointer). Impact: Opus (4), one small commit.

203. **Order after round 18: M4's remaining exit items, who holds each, and the ranked hand-out (`git log origin/main` at `9d401984`; round 18).** State: since §17.198, landed on `main`: M4-OPT (`f93e7ab7`..`08030b43`, `f54f8276`), the deep-IR guard and `V4004` (`5e1bcafb`..`e6b26437`), WS-P11a and the WS-P11b record (`71625ab7`, `d2e0fc3d`), the WS-P9 review packet (`6aec3c1c`), §17.194's determinism (`96b8d5d3`, `7f6ec255`), §17.196's path and `*.snap` closures (`31916445`, `9d401984`). 204. **Compile latency after WS-C1: the three as-built readings are ratified, the reuse path carries std's results instead of walking its trees (WS-C1b), std's trees are parsed on demand (WS-C1c), WS-C2 persists the analysis and not the trees, interface hashing gets its row (WS-C1d), the process floor is attributed (WS-C3), and the first analysis goes std-first once the walk is gone (WS-C1 `4b31d6d0`, `bde235ca`; `research/10-evidence/compile-latency-r1.md`; plan `performance-r1.md` §9; round 19).** Facts (the research note; loaded host, indicative, §17.153): one `sakura check` is unchanged by WS-C1, 25.1 ms on the hello-world and 61.0 ms on the 10k corpus against targets of 10 and 45; in process a reusing analysis costs 11.8 ms against 22.4, and the 11.8 is check 3.9, `symbols::index` 3.4, `interface_hash` 2.2, `evaluated_functions` 1.3 and lower 1.0, every one a walk of std's 45 trees for a result std's own analysis already holds; 3.8 ms of the process falls outside `analyze` and `sakura --version` is 3.5 ms; WS-C2 with the trees reparsed and the walk kept projects to about 19 ms, 15 with WS-C1a. The budget is therefore: 10 ms less the ~3.8 outside `analyze` leaves ~6 ms for the cache read, std's reuse and the hello module (≤ 1 ms). Decision: (1) **The three readings are ratified.** (a; the flip lands with WS-C2, §17.214 (1)) *Std is analysed at a process's second analysis, not its first*: right while the reuse path walks std (41 against 23 ms for a one-off `check`), and the rule flips with (2): once a reusing analysis costs under 3 ms, the first analysis in a process is std-first too (`std_analysis()` on the first call, from the cache once WS-C2 has one), so one path serves `check`, the LSP and the MCP server and §17.195 (2)'s "computed once per process on first use" reads literally again; acceptance for the flip is fresh-first and std-first within 1 ms on the hello-world in process, and the determinism test keeps its fully fresh side through the crate-internal `analyze(.., [])`. (b) *Reuse from the same files or from std alone, never from a run of user files*: a prefix is reusable only when it is closed under imports, and without a dependency graph the only prefix known to be closed is std (it imports nothing from the user package); a user prefix can hold a `V1xxx` unresolved import that a file past the prefix resolves. In §17.74's terms the predicate is "the same files, or an import-closed prefix", and std is the only import-closed prefix until a per-project cache (§17.195's later item) brings a graph. (c) *Interface hashes compared only for files whose text changed*: ratified and taken one step further. An interface hash answers one question, whether an edit to this file changed what dependents can see, so it is **computed** only for a file whose text changed or that has no predecessor; an unchanged file carries its previous hash. §17.195 (2)'s "equal by URI, content hash and interface hash" reads "equal by URI and content hash, or by interface hash where the text changed". The known conservative case: a hash carried under one evaluated-name set and compared with one computed under another can mismatch spuriously, which costs a fresh analysis and never a wrong reuse; the `std_prefix` tests and the fresh-against-reused corpus comparison stay the predicate's check. (2) **WS-C1b: the reuse path carries, it does not recompute** (landed `38f376b3`; reads as built with per-file call facts and a fixpoint, §17.214 (3); Opus; `sakura_analysis/src/{lib,symbols}.rs`, `sakura_hir/src/lower.rs`; after WS-C1a, before WS-C2). A reused module's results are its predecessor's: `symbols::index` indexes only the modules that were lowered and appends them after the predecessor's entries for the reused prefix (std-first ordering makes std's symbols a prefix as its `FileId`s are; `Symbol` is plain data, so the carry is a clone or a shared slice); the evaluated names are kept per file in `Analyzed` and an analysis's set is the union of the carried sets and the changed files' fresh ones (§17.87's by-name rule unchanged); interface hashes per (1c); and `lower_reusing`'s item collection and import binding for a reused module read the predecessor `Program`'s items, not the tree (the per-module lowering §17.74 asked for, finished). Acceptance: on the hello-world in process (the research note's loop) a reusing analysis ≤ 3 ms with WS-C1a (11.8 today, 8.4 after C1a, about 1.5 expected) and each carried phase ≤ 0.1 ms on the phase lines; `std_reuse_matches_a_fresh_analysis` and §17.194's order test green with the symbols compared too (`sakura doc --search` and the LSP's workspace symbols over std byte-identical fresh and reused); `recheck.rs`'s counters unchanged. (3) **WS-C1c: std's trees are parsed on demand** (Opus; `sakura_analysis/src/lib.rs` `Analyzed::parse`, `specs.rs`; with or right after (2), before WS-C2). After (2) nothing in a reusing analysis reads a reused file's tree, so `Analyzed.parses` holds `None` for a reused std file and `Analyzed::parse(file)` parses it on first use (`parse_cached` by content hash, as today) for navigation, hover, doc and frozen specs; the parse cache stays per process. Acceptance: a reusing analysis performs no std parse (`Stats.parses` on a second program counts user files only); `sakura doc`, LSP hover and goto into std, and every `--dump` unchanged. (4) **WS-C2 persists the analysis, not the trees.** The file holds std's `Program`, `TypedProgram` (interner, schemes, typed bodies), `Symbol`s with their `file` and `def` (which the JSON derive skips; a cache-side derive or wrapper), the per-file interface hashes and evaluated-name sets, and the snapshot's file hashes; no syntax tree. A fresh process then reads and verifies the file, deserialises, runs (2)'s reuse path over the user files and analyses the user modules. Budget per step on the hello-world, measured on the phase lines and recorded in the research note before the quiet-window run: deserialise ≤ 2 ms (the serde binary crate §17.195 (3) names; if it measures over 2 ms the note says where, and a zero-copy format is decided then, not now), reuse path ≤ 1.5 ms, the hello module ≤ 1 ms, outside `analyze` ≤ 4 ms. Starts after WS-C1a, (2) and (3) are on `main` ((3)'s `parses: None` is the shape the file assumes); acceptance as §17.195 (3) states it plus these lines. (5) **WS-C1d: interface hashing gets its own row.** It is 14% of the 10k corpus's analysis (7.7 ms against parse's 8.8), under §17.195 (4)'s quarter, but after (1c) and (4) it is the one phase left that re-reads what parsing just produced, it falls entirely on the fresh user files `check_cold_10k` measures (5.5 of the 7.7 ms is the user package), and the projected 10k time after WS-C2 (about 41 ms) leaves the 45 ms target 4 ms of margin. Opus, small, after WS-C2; `sakura_analysis/src/lib.rs` `interface_hash`: one pass that skips body subtrees without materialising a red node per token (the green tree or the token stream, whichever the parser exposes cheaply). Acceptance: `interface_hash` ≤ a quarter of parse on the 10k corpus (≤ 2.2 ms) on the phase lines; hash values may change (nothing persists them across builds; WS-C2's key names the build); every `std_prefix` and `recheck` test green. (6) **WS-C3: the process floor is attributed** (Astra measures, with WS-C2's measurement; Opus fixes what it finds; a `research/10-evidence` note). `sakura --version` at 3.5 ms and 3.8 ms outside `analyze` are a third of the 10 ms target: attribute them (dynamic loading and static initialisers, the project read, the sha256 over std's 45 embedded files for the snapshot hash, which is computable once per process, and the output) with timestamps or a sampler; anything above a trivial Rust binary's start on the host that is not the project read is fixed where it is. Acceptance: `--version` and `check_hello`'s share outside `analyze` recorded in the note; a fix lands only with a before and after. **Ranked by ms saved per effort** (hello-world, fresh process, after WS-C2): WS-C1a, check, 3.3 ms, small, in flight; WS-C1b, symbols 3.4 + hashes 2.2 + names 1.2 + lower 0.8 = 7.6 ms, medium; WS-C1c, parse 3.25 ms, small once (2) has landed; WS-C3, up to about 2 ms, measure first; WS-C1d, 5.5 ms on the 10k corpus only, small. Projection: `check_hello` ≈ 3.8 + 2 + 1.5 + 1 ≈ 8.3 ms; `check_cold_10k` ≈ 41 ms before WS-C1d. Why: the 10 ms target is a budget for the work a hello-world needs, which is reading one module and knowing std, and a cache followed by a walk of what it cached is not a cache. Rejected: WS-C2 alone (projected 15–19 ms); a zero-copy format now (decided on a measured deserialise); skipping Core lowering in `check` (§17.200 (6) lowers user modules only, so no std cost sits there); lowering lazily (reused modules are already not lowered; what remained is item collection, which (2) takes from the predecessor); a user-program cache this round (§17.195). Performance: this entry is the measured item; numbers enter `bench/deltas.md` only from a quiet window (§17.153). Changed: §17.195 (2) (pointer), plan `performance-r1.md` §9 (WS-C1 ratified, WS-C2 restated, rows WS-C1b, WS-C1c, WS-C1d, WS-C3, order after round 19), plan `m3-completion-and-m4.md` §3.8 rows 13 and 14. Impact: Opus (WS-C1a–d, WS-C2), Astra (WS-C3, the quiet window), the owner (promotion of targets to budgets).

205. **`read_body` is linear and stays as written; a recorded streamed body is at most 16 MiB; `Http.send` on JS gets the WASM host's response budget; `Bytes.concat` waits for a second caller (REQUESTS 2026-10-19 "Opus (std, small items)"; gate 10 finding C2; §17.191 (5); round 19).** Facts (the REQUESTS entry; release build, loaded host, indicative): recording adds 0.32 s at 4 MiB and 1.00 s at 16 MiB, 3.1× for 4× the bytes, so linear: `List.concat(out, chunk)` pushes onto the newest version and both runtimes' persistent push is amortised O(1); about 15 bytes of RSS per body byte (one boxed `Int` per byte); no limit of its own, bounded only by the test runner's 1 GB V8 heap, which the measuring test reaches at 32 MiB; a single-loop rewrite measured the same; the path runs only under `SAKURA_RECORD=1` on a cassette miss, and `--record` refuses `--target wasm`. Found while answering: JS `http_send` reads a whole response with `r.text()` and no limit, while the WASM host caps copied bytes at `SAKURA_MAX_COPY_BYTES` (256 MiB, `Err("response too large")`, ABI §8.5) and `serveHttp` caps a request body at 1 MiB (§17.159 (6)). Decision: (1) **C2 closes as measured linear; "quadratic" is withdrawn** by a dated erratum in the review record's C2 row (the signature covers the state reviewed, the erratum the correction) and a pointer in §17.191 (5). "Unbounded" stood and is closed by (2). (2) **A recorded streamed body is at most 16 MiB (16,777,216 bytes)** (Opus, std, small; `std/http.sak` `read_body` and the `recording` doc comment; `sakura_cli/tests/cli.rs`'s `--record` case). `read_body` counts `Bytes.len(chunk)` and, before the next read past the limit, fails with `HttpError { status: None, reason: "response body over 16 MiB: sakura test --record stores at most 16,777,216 bytes of a streamed body" }`, so at most the limit plus one 64 KiB chunk is ever held and nothing is appended to the cassette (the append runs only once the body is whole). Why 16 MiB: at 15 bytes per byte it is 240 MB under the runner's 1 GB heap with room for the test's own data, the measured cost is one second, a cassette is a checked-in file holding the body as text or base64, and 16 MiB is the number the std cache file already uses (§17.195 (3)); not 1 MiB (responses are larger than requests, and `Http.stream` exists for large ones), and not an option (nobody has asked; the error names the limit). Acceptance: the entry's own setup (a package test recording one `Http.scripted` body) with 16 MiB + 1 byte reports the `HttpError` naming the limit and leaves the cassette without that exchange; 16 MiB exactly records; the doc comment and `std/INDEX.md` name the limit. (3) **`Http.send` on JS reads at most `SAKURA_MAX_COPY_BYTES` (default 256 MiB)**, the WASM host's budget, variable and message, as `Err("response too large")` under the `Result<_, String>` policy of §17.199 (5) (Opus, runtime/js, small; `sakura_runtime.js` `http_send`: a declared `content-length` over the limit is refused before the body is read, and the body is read through its reader with a running count otherwise; `runtime/js/ABI.md` §7, a PATCH erratum since an existing `Err` case gains a cause; test: a local server answering more than a limit set low through the variable is an `HttpError` with that reason and the fetch is aborted). `http_stream_read` needs nothing: each call is bounded by `max`. The rule is AGENTS.md's explicit resource limits; one number shared with the WASM host is one fewer to explain. (4) **`Bytes.concat(List<Bytes>) -> Bytes` is not added now.** With (2) the remaining cost is one second and 240 MB once per recorded exchange on a developer's machine, while the builtin costs an ABI MINOR, `builtins.sigs`, the WASM table and both hosts for a path WASM never reaches under `--record`. It lands when a second std function needs to join chunks (a whole-body helper over `Http.stream`, chunked `Fs` reads), then as a universal builtin on both backends with `read_body` as its second user; recorded as a pending std question, not a row. Why: a finding is corrected by the measurement that disproves it; the project rule is explicit limits, and the `Int`-per-byte representation makes the limit necessary at a smaller number than the heap. Rejected: a builtin for one caller; closing "unbounded" as accepted (the rule); the limit in `http_recording_append_stream` (too late, the bytes are already held). Changed: `docs/reviews/m3-security-review.md` C2 (erratum), §17.191 (5) (pointer), REQUESTS (the answer), plan `m3-completion-and-m4.md` §3.8 (row 19). Impact: Opus (std; runtime/js), two small commits; Sol none; the owner none. Follow-ups after landing: §17.209.

206. **Order after round 19: the latency rows are one sequence in one file, the two limits go now, and everything else in §17.203 stands (`git log origin/main` at `6d91e3c0`; round 19).** State: since §17.203, WS-C1 landed (`4b31d6d0`, `bde235ca`), §17.187 (`9a5c8ea1`) and the `read_body` measurement (`848f4945`); WS-C1a is in flight (`sakura_types/src/check/mod.rs`). New this round, ranked, each one commit: **1** WS-C1a (Opus, in flight); **2** §17.204 (2) WS-C1b (Opus; `sakura_analysis/src/{lib,symbols}.rs`, `sakura_hir/src/lower.rs`; after 1); **3** §17.204 (3) WS-C1c (Opus; `sakura_analysis/src/lib.rs`, `specs.rs`; with or after 2); **4** WS-C2 as restated in §17.204 (4) (Opus; after 2 and 3; files as plan §9 lists them); **5** §17.205 (2) the recorded-body limit (Opus, std, small; `std/http.sak`, `std/INDEX.md`, `sakura_cli/tests/cli.rs`; now, no collision); **6** §17.205 (3) the JS `http_send` budget (Opus, runtime/js, small; `sakura_runtime.js`, `runtime/js/ABI.md`, a runtime test; now, no collision with §17.200's `emit.rs` or `sakura_wasm_host.js` items); **7** §17.204 (5) WS-C1d (Opus, small; after 4); **8** §17.204 (6) WS-C3 (Astra, with 4's measurement; a fix by Opus only with a before and after); **9** §17.204 (1a)'s flip to std-first at the first analysis (Opus, one line and the test; with 2 or 3, once 2's acceptance holds). Collisions: 1 is in `sakura_types`; 2, 3, 4, 7 and 9 all edit `sakura_analysis/src/lib.rs` and run strictly in that order under one owner; 2 also edits `sakura_hir/src/lower.rs`, which no §17.203 item touches; 5 and 6 touch std and the JS runtime only. §17.203's ranked list is otherwise unchanged, including the tree-sitter grammar (item 14, now without `read_body`, which is 5 here). Why: the latency rows share one file, so their order is the only parallelism question; the limits are the project rule and small. Rejected: starting WS-C2 before 2 and 3 (it would persist the trees or a shape (3) then changes). Changed: plan `performance-r1.md` §9 (order after round 19), plan `m3-completion-and-m4.md` §3.8 (rows 13, 14, 19). Impact: Opus, Astra as named; Sol none; the owner none.

207. **Compile latency after WS-C1a: a syntax error suppresses only the diagnostics inside its own body, std's test blocks stay in every analysis, and the type interner's hasher is a measured row after WS-C1d (WS-C1a `1c675148`, `b0dfe8b1`; `research/10-evidence/compile-latency-r1.md` "WS-C1a"; round 20).** Facts (the research note; loaded host, indicative, §17.153): the reuse path's check fell from 4.39 to 0.63 ms and a reusing analysis from 12.55 to 8.66 ms; a fresh `sakura check` is unchanged. `Checker::push` (`sakura_types/src/check/mod.rs`) drops every diagnostic raised while `b.suppress` holds, and `with_body` sets `b.suppress` when a lex or parse diagnostic falls inside the body's range; a signature is lowered lazily at its first use (`scheme_of` → `sig_type`, `decls.rs`), so a bad signature of another module first used inside such a body pushes through the same `push` and is lost, although its span is in the other module. Probe: `app`'s test `{ let x = lib.f()\n let y = (1 + }` with `lib`'s `pub fn f() -> List<Int, Int>` gives `[V0001, V2010]` fresh and `[V0001, V2002, V2010]` reusing (the reusing analysis skips the body, so `V2002` is raised at `f`'s own check); `refine.rs` copies the same flag into a pending literal and drops its `V5004` the same way. std's 109 `test` blocks are 46.3% of std's check (`check_test_like`), and a user package's `sakura test` runs std's tests only under `--std` or when the snapshot has no user files (`testing::only_std`). The sampling profile: hashing 22% of self time (SipHash `write` 12.7%), `Interner::intern` 15.2% and `Interner::row` 10.6% inclusive, `Type::clone` 10.4%; the interner's maps use `std::hash::RandomState`; `rustc-hash` 2.1.3 and `foldhash` 0.2.0 are already in `compiler/Cargo.lock` as transitive dependencies; `sakura_types` depends on `la-arena`, `text-size` and two workspace crates. Decision: (1) **Rule (the "no cascades" rule `check_program_with` documents, sharpened): suppression is by place, not by time.** A diagnostic is a cascade of a syntax error if and only if its primary span lies inside the body that holds the error (same file, within the body's range); a diagnostic whose primary span is anywhere else (another module's signature, the def's own signature, a type alias) is reported where it is first raised. Fix (Opus, small; `sakura_types/src/check/{mod,body,refine}.rs`; no `sakura_analysis/src` file, so beside WS-C1b): `b.suppress` becomes the suppressed range (file, lo, hi) or none, `push` drops a diagnostic only when its primary span is inside it, and a pending literal keeps its bool, computed from its own range when queued. Fresh and reusing analyses then agree on `[V0001, V2002, V2010]`. Acceptance: the probe as a two-module fixture in `compiler/tests/ui` with both orders of first use (the bad signature used first from the broken body, and first from a sound one); `sakura_analysis/tests/reused_tests.rs` asserts fresh and reusing results equal on it; `std_reuse_matches_a_fresh_analysis` green; every existing `tests/ui` cascade fixture unchanged except where a def's own signature error was hidden by its body's syntax error, which is now reported and the commit names the fixture. Why: the fresh output was the wrong one, and the rule says what a cascade is instead of when it happened. Rejected: lowering declarations with suppression off (covers signatures only; the span test also covers `V5004` and any later lazily raised diagnostic); making the reusing side agree with the fresh one (drops a real error). (2) **std's test blocks are checked in every analysis; there is no test-less mode.** The saving is about 3.5 ms per fresh std analysis (46% of 7.55 ms), and after WS-C2 a fresh std analysis runs once per compiler build per machine: `check`, `build`, the LSP and the MCP server take std from the cache, so the hot path saves nothing. A test-less analysis would be a second shape of std's analysis: `sakura test --std` lowers std's tests from the same cached analysis, which a test-less cache could not serve, and `std_reuse_matches_a_fresh_analysis` compares the full one. The rule, should a fresh std analysis return to a measured hot path (a cache-less environment; none exists today): std's test blocks are needed only when std is the package under test (`--std` or `only_std`), the predicate is "the snapshot has user files and `std_tests` is off", it skips `check_test_like` for std files only, and the cache stays the full analysis. Effect on WS-C2: none; the file holds the full analysis as §17.204 (4) states it. (3) **WS-C1e: the interner hashes with FxHash, measured, after WS-C1d** (Opus, small; the `Interner` in `sakura_types`, workspace `Cargo.toml` gaining `rustc-hash` as a workspace dependency per `compiler/AGENTS.md`, `sakura_types/Cargo.toml`). `HashMap<Type, TypeId>` and the row map take `BuildHasherDefault<FxHasher>`. Why FxHash and not `foldhash` or `ahash`: deterministic by construction (no random state), no dependencies of its own, already compiled in this workspace, and what rustc uses for the same shape (interned IR keys); within AGENTS.md's dependency rule because it is measured, transitive already, and replaces a standard hasher rather than adding a capability. Determinism is untouched: `TypeId`s are allocation-ordered and neither map is iterated into output (§17.194's order test and the snapshot byte parity are in the acceptance). Hash flooding: the keys are the program's own types, bounded by source size and the parser's depth bound, so a crafted program can only slow its own check, which §17.195's budgets catch; not a trust boundary. Why after WS-C1d: after WS-C2 only the user package's check is on the hot path (about 9.5 of the 10k corpus's 17.0 ms), hashing is about 2 ms of it, and WS-C1d's 5.5 ms comes first. Acceptance: the `check_loop` profile before and after in the research note; `check` on the 10k corpus at least 8% faster on the phase lines, or the row closes as measured and unlanded (as WS-P11b did, §17.201); no `TypeId` changes (the interner tests); workspace checks green. `Type::clone` (10%, 63 `.ty(..).clone()` sites) is not a row: a borrowing refactor of the readers is the next candidate only if `check_cold_10k` is still over 45 ms at the quiet window after WS-C1e. Why: the one correctness finding gets a rule and a one-place fix; the two perf leads are weighed against what stays on the hot path after WS-C2, and only one survives. Rejected: a test-less std analysis (two shapes for 3.5 ms once per build); `ahash` (random state for no gain here); hashing changes before WS-C1d (smaller win, same target). Changed: plan `performance-r1.md` §9 (rows WS-C1e and the suppression fix; order after round 20); `sakura_types/src/lib.rs`'s `check_program_with` doc reads as (1) (the implementer). Impact: Opus (the fix, WS-C1e); Astra (the WS-C1e profile beside the quiet window); the owner none. **(1) landed as `380b4efd` with "any span" in place of "primary span"; ratified in §17.221.**

208. **WS-P9b readings: the dispatch join is an `if` chain until §17.200 (2)'s literal `Switch` lands, the nested-`if` return-join fix belongs to the WS-P9 sign-off, and a tail-call clique joins the benchmark suite (`72bb3829`, `527a131b`, `a95cb7f8`; §17.192 (2); round 20).** Facts: Core's `SwitchOn` has `Variant` and `Errors` only (`ir.rs`), so the dispatch join's body is an `if` chain on `state == k`, one arm per copied member, each arm a join with one jump into it; `MERGE_MEMBERS` = 4 bounds k, so a cross-member jump pays at most three compares. `return_joins` recorded only joins whose code is `ret v`, so a tail call under `else if`, or under any `if` nested in an `if` branch, stayed a call, for self-recursion as much as for cycles; it now also records a join that forwards one of its parameters to a return join. Every corpus program loses blocks and keeps its dup and drop counts; rbtree's all-frame WASM run takes 21,969 frames instead of 22,054 and no other counter moved. The packet (`docs/reviews/ws-p9-tail-loops.md`) has both as 1(f) and 1(g), the fixture rows in §2 (`tail_calls_under_nested_ifs_become_jumps`, `mutual_tail_dispatch`, `mutual_tail_dispatch_blame`) and one sign-off row covering both. Measured by the implementer on a loaded host (indicative): a three-def clique at 10^7 steps, JS 1,096 ms and 1 GiB → 103 ms and 47 MiB, WASM 3,421 → 196 ms. No program in `bench/programs` holds a tail-call cycle; `micro.sak` has none either (packet §3). WS-B is building the WASM lane in `bench/harness` now (§17.191 (2)). Decision: (1) **§17.192 (2) reads as built**: the host's loop holds one dispatch join whose body decides on an `Int` state local with one arm per member body; the form is the `if` chain today. Once §17.200 (2) lands `SwitchOn::Lit`, the dispatch becomes one `Switch` on the state (Opus, small; `sakura_core/src/opt.rs` only; after plan `m3-completion-and-m4.md` §3.8 row 16's first commit): acceptance is the `mutual_tail_dispatch*` fixtures' expected output, frames and blame spans unchanged, `--dump direct` showing one `Switch` per host, and the snapshot prints reviewed as that change and nothing else. Why: one way in Core to decide on a literal, and the flat JS `switch` and WASM index arithmetic it buys; not worth building twice, so it waits for the enum. The swap is for uniformity and is measured on (3)'s program; nothing requires it. (2) **The nested-`if` fix is part of WS-P9 and of its sign-off.** It was needed by WS-P9b and it changes which calls are tail calls for plain self-recursion too: an `else if` tail call that ran through frames now loops. That is the §17.160 (5) rule reaching a shape it missed, not a new rule: a program that overflowed now runs, traces are equal, and no counter but rbtree's moved. The owner's sign-off row "WS-P9b dispatch join and nested-`if` return joins (sections 1(f), 1(g) and 2)" covers it; nothing else is owed, and this entry is the design record the brief did not have. (3) **`tail_clique` joins the suite** (WS-B, in its lane; `bench/programs/tail_clique/{meta.toml,sakura/main.sak,js/main.mjs}`; ports later by the WS-S protocol): family "self-tail and mutual recursion" beside `countdown`; three top-level defs each calling both others in tail position, `run(n)` a checksum of the states visited over `n` steps; sizes 10^5, 10^6, 10^7 (103 ms on JS at 10^7); lane `matched`, where the control writes the state machine as a loop, which is what the merged Core is, so the cells compare the dispatch. Acceptance: `bench oracle --write` records the checksums; `bench run --suite js --filter tail_clique` and the `wasm` suite produce records whose `frames` counter is small; `--dump direct` on it shows the dispatch host; no baseline is blessed (the owner, at a window, §17.153). Why: §17.109's suite is where a regression in the transform shows (the frames counter and the time), no program exercises the dispatch, and plan §5 item 4's 2× code-size rule finally has a cycle to read against. Rejected: adding a cycle to `micro.sak` (the code-size probe, not a runtime bench); waiting for the native ports (the Sakura and JS cells show the counter). Changed: §17.192 (2) (pointer), plan `performance-r1.md` §9 (WS-P9b done; the `Switch` follow-up; the `tail_clique` row). Impact: Opus (the follow-up), WS-B (the program), the owner (one sign-off row, already in the packet).

209. **HTTP limits after §17.205: `SAKURA_MAX_COPY_BYTES` is a positive integer or a refusal, never unlimited; a body cut short is `Err` on both hosts and never an unhandled rejection; the WASM budget is a per-instance ceiling and is documented as one; a recorded `send` body gets the 16 MiB cassette limit too (`87aca654`, `15ca4293`; §17.205; round 20).** Facts: both hosts read the variable as `Number(env ?? default)` (`sakura_runtime.js` `maxCopyBytes`, per request; `sakura_wasm_host.js` `MAX_COPY_BYTES`, once at load): a non-numeric value is `NaN`, every `>` test against it is false and the limit is off; an empty value is `0` and every body with bytes is refused. The JS runtime maps a rejection of a suspended host promise to `D091` (`resumeLater`, `hostFailure`), so a connection dropped while `boundedText` reads the body is a defect, while `http_stream_read` maps the same failure to `Err(failureOf(e))` and a failed `fetch` is `Err`. The WASM host's `async` import does `r.p.then(reply)` with no rejection handler: a drop mid-body, or `cancel(task)` aborting the fetch through `inflight`, rejects `r.p` unhandled, which Node turns into a process exit. ABI §8.5 already says the budget counts one answer together with the completions queued for delivery, and `copyOut` counts the encoded answer, headers included, so a body within the limit by less than that is `Ok` on JS and `Err` on WASM. Under `--record`, `send`'s body is read whole by the host up to the copy budget and appended whole to the cassette; `read_body`'s 16 MiB applies to streams only. Decision: (1) **Rule for every byte-count variable** (`SAKURA_MAX_COPY_BYTES` now; `SAKURA_MAX_HEAP` and `SAKURA_MAX_REQUESTS` when a host reads them from the environment): unset is the default; a string of decimal digits with value at least 1 is that value; anything else (empty, signs, fractions, exponents, spaces, words) is a configuration error, refused once at first use, before any body is read, with the variable, the value seen and the accepted form in the message, so a limit is never off and never zero by accident. Fix (Opus, small; one commit): `sakura_runtime.js` gains one helper, `bytesEnv(name, default)`, memoised, throwing `Defect("D091", "SAKURA_MAX_COPY_BYTES must be a positive integer number of bytes, got \"…\"")`; `http_send` and the WASM host's budget both read it at first use (the host already imports the runtime; its constant becomes a lazy read so a bad value is a reported defect, not a load-time crash). Why a defect: both hosts have the path, it is uncatchable so no program runs past a broken limit, and `sakura test` reports it the same way on both backends. Acceptance: runtime tests for `abc`, the empty string, `0`, `-1`, `1.5`, `1e3` and `" 42"` give the defect, `42` gives 42 and unset gives 256 MiB; `http_send_over_the_copy_budget_fails_alike_on_both_backends` gains an invalid value with the same defect on both backends; ABI §8.5 and `runtime/js/ABI.md` state the rule (a PATCH erratum: a failure mode specified, no shape changes); `std/INDEX.md` names it; a dated note under C2 in `docs/reviews/m3-security-review.md` records that the limit failed open on an invalid value and fails closed from this commit. (2) **A connection dropped while `send` reads the body is `Err(reason)` on both hosts, and the WASM `async` import always settles its request.** The ABI §8.5 policy decides it: an operation whose result is a `Result<_, String>` answers `Err` with the message, as `http_stream_read` and a failed `fetch` already do; a cut body is an ordinary network failure a program retries, not a host defect, and a truncated `Ok` is never returned. This is a semantics change on JS (`D091` today) and a crash fix on WASM; it lands as one commit, because the rejection handler is the fix and what it answers is the semantics, with the owner's review requested in the commit message as AGENTS.md asks for semantics changes (CODEOWNERS: `/compiler/` @icantcodefyi). Fix (Opus, small): `http_send` reads the body inside the promise chain's failure handling so a body read that rejects is `Err(failureOf(e))`; `sakura_wasm_host.js` `async` passes a rejection handler to `r.p.then`: a request no longer in `inflight` (cancelled) is dropped silently (`complete` already ignores it), an uncancelled one gets `Err(failureOf(e))` for a `Result` schema and `defectAnswer(D091)` otherwise. Acceptance: a runtime test with a local server that sends part of a body and closes gives `Err` on JS; the same program on both backends through the CLI test gives the same output; a WASM test that cancels a task while its `send` reads a body ends without an unhandled rejection (Node's default `--unhandled-rejections` turns one into a failure, which is the check); ABI §8.5's as-built sentence gains "a body cut short is `Err`"; CHANGELOG. (3) **The near-limit difference stands and is documented.** The budget is a ceiling on host memory, not a byte-exact contract: JS counts body bytes per response, the WASM host counts the encoded answer plus the completions queued per instance, so a response within the limit by less than its headers and the other in-flight answers can be `Ok` on one host and `Err` on the other. No program observes the threshold except by setting it, and the parity test stays far past it (4 KiB against 1 KiB). One sentence in `std/INDEX.md` ("at most about") and one in ABI §8.5. Rejected: counting headers on JS (parity on a number nobody may depend on, and a per-instance aggregate on JS needs an in-flight ledger the JS runtime does not keep). (4) **A recorded `send` body is at most 16 MiB too** (Opus, std, small; `std/http.sak` `replaying`'s `send` arm, `std/INDEX.md`, `sakura_cli/tests/cli.rs`). §17.205 (2)'s reason that a cassette is a checked-in file applies to any body; its heap reason does not (the body is a string bounded by the copy budget), so the check sits at append time, which was too late for a stream and is right here: before `http_recording_append`, a `resp.body` over 16,777,216 UTF-8 bytes is `fail HttpError { status: None, reason: "response body over 16 MiB: sakura test --record stores at most 16,777,216 bytes of a response body" }` and nothing is appended; the streamed message reads the same ("of a response body" replaces "of a streamed body"; the text is a day old and pre-alpha), in `read_body`, `INDEX.md` and the skill reference. Replay of an existing larger cassette is not checked. Acceptance: the `--record` CLI test gains a `send` body of 16 MiB + 1 byte (the `HttpError`, no exchange appended) and one of exactly 16 MiB (recorded). Why: a limit that reads from the environment must fail closed; the ABI's own policy answers the drop; the budget's purpose is memory, not a number; the cassette limit is about the artifact, so one number for both paths. Rejected: an exit instead of a defect for (1) (the defect reaches both backends' reporting unchanged); landing the WASM handler with `D091` first and `Err` later (one more semantics the review would read twice); a cassette size option (nobody has asked; the message names the limit). Changed: §17.205 (pointer), plan `m3-completion-and-m4.md` §3.8 (row 19 pointer, row 20), `runtime/rt/ABI.md` §8.5 and `runtime/js/ABI.md` (errata by the implementer), `docs/reviews/m3-security-review.md` C2 (dated note). Impact: Opus (three small commits), the owner (review of (2)'s commit), Sol none. **Landed as `cfe90b87`, `187a8f80`, `cffce484`; three deviations ratified and the serve crash routed in §17.216.**

210. **Order after round 20: the host fixes lead, the suppression rule goes beside WS-C1b, and everything else in §17.206 stands (`git log origin/main` at `15ca4293`; round 20).** State: since §17.206, landed on `main`: WS-C1a (`1c675148`, `b0dfe8b1`), the nested-`if` return joins and WS-P9b with the packet (`72bb3829`, `527a131b`, `a95cb7f8`), §17.205's two limits (`87aca654`, `15ca4293`). In flight: WS-C1b (Opus; `sakura_analysis/src/{lib,symbols}.rs`, `sakura_hir/src/lower.rs`), the WASM bench lane (Opus WS-B; `bench/harness`). New this round, ranked, each one commit: **1** §17.209 (1) the fail-closed budget variable (Opus, small; `runtime/js/sakura_runtime.js`, `sakura_wasm_host.js`, `runtime/js/test`, `sakura_cli/tests/wasm.rs`, both ABI documents, `std/INDEX.md`, the C2 note) — a limit that is off on a typo is the project rule broken, so first; **2** §17.209 (2) the cut body and the WASM rejection handler (Opus, small, same files, owner review requested in the message; after 1, same functions); **3** §17.207 (1) the suppression rule (Opus, small; `sakura_types/src/check/{mod,body,refine}.rs`, a `compiler/tests/ui` fixture, `sakura_analysis/tests/reused_tests.rs`; now, beside WS-C1b, which owns `sakura_analysis/src` and `sakura_hir/src/lower.rs` and is not touched); **4** §17.209 (4) the recorded `send` body (Opus, std, small; `std/http.sak`, `std/INDEX.md`, `skills/sakura/references/stdlib.md`, `sakura_cli/tests/cli.rs`; now, colliding with nothing); **5** §17.208 (3) `tail_clique` (WS-B, in its lane, with the `wasm` suite or right after it); **6** §17.207 (3) WS-C1e (Opus, small; `sakura_types`, workspace `Cargo.toml`; after WS-C1d, so after the whole §17.206 sequence); **7** §17.208 (1) the dispatch as `SwitchOn::Lit` (Opus, small; `sakura_core/src/opt.rs`; after plan §3.8 row 16's first commit). §17.206's ranked list is otherwise unchanged: WS-C1b, WS-C1c, WS-C2, WS-C1d, WS-C3, the std-first flip, in that order under one owner. Collisions: 1 and 2 share the two host files and run in order; 3 is `sakura_types` only; 4 is std only; 5 is `bench/**`; 6 and 7 wait on their predecessors' files. Why: the two host fixes are the resource-limit rule and a process crash; the suppression rule corrects a wrong fresh output and sits in a crate nobody else is editing; the rest are small closures of this round's questions. Rejected: holding 3 for WS-C1b (different crates); holding 2 for a review before landing (the owner reviews on `main`, as every semantics commit this month). Changed: plan `performance-r1.md` §9 (order after round 20), plan `m3-completion-and-m4.md` §3.8 (row 20). Impact: Opus, WS-B as named; the owner (one review, one sign-off row); Sol none.

211. **The WASM lane's first reading: handlers, callbacks and allocation are the three causes, two of them are M4-OPT's unbuilt half, and WS-W1 and WS-W2 become M4 exit items (WS-B `543571d1`..`daa50f1c`; `bench/results/2026-10-19-wsb-wasm-advisory.{json,md}`; plan `m3-completion-and-m4.md` §7 L4 item 2; round 21).** Facts (advisory, loaded host, §17.153; the ratios are Sakura-WASM over hand JS on Node, which no ceiling names, the M4 envelope being against the native ports): the median ratio is 47.5 on Node and 79.8 on Wasmtime; `fib` 1.2–2.8, `countdown` 1.7–3.1, `tail_clique` 2.6–3.2, `binary_trees` 7–14, `map_set` and `tokenizer` 4–22, and `handled_d1`/`_d4`/`_d16`/`_fwd`/`_suspend` 67–210, `contracts_plain` 163–322, `contracts` 215–375, `nbody` 74–108, `deltablue` 309–608. The `frames` counter (`bench_counters.txt`, identical in the WASM records) names the cause per program. (a) **Every `perform` is a frame on WASM.** `handled_d1` runs 1,005 frames per 1,000 operations, with and without twins: `sakura_lir::lower` emits every `Bind::Perform` as `Term::Perform` (a block end, `rt_perform`, a continuation frame), and `direct::regions` refuses any function holding `HandlePush`, `StateGet`/`StateSet` or `MakeCallback`, so §17.113 (a)'s lexical binding, whose facts the LIR computes (`direct.rs` agrees with `--dump direct`), is never used by the lowering, and §17.113 (b)'s tail-direct dispatch has no WASM form. (b) **Every callback of a callback-taking builtin is a frame and a box.** `contracts_plain`'s 1,121 frames at n = 1,000 are 62 `List.fold` calls × (16 elements + 2) + 5, and its 1,070 boxes the fold's `Int` accumulator boxed per callback (ABI §4); `nbody`'s 7 per step are `List.map`'s five callbacks and two calls; `pipeline` (2,508), `shapes` (5,010) and `map_set` (2,005) read the same way. A lambda has no twin (plan §7 L4 item 2 left "lambdas, clauses, bound performs and closure calls of function-typed parameters" to M4-OPT, whose items 1–8 never took them), and the runtime's list builtins can call a closure only through `rt_call_value` frames. `contracts` adds one frame per `increment` call over its companion (2,175 against 1,121); WS-W2's first step reads `--dump direct` on it and names the reason. (c) **A frame costs 150–200 ns on this host** (`frame_alloc`: a heap object, an arena slot, the driver round trip, two reference-count operations and the free) against 1.3–1.8 ns per iteration of the hand JS, so a program with one frame per iteration is 100× whatever else it does. (d) **The rest is allocation.** `nbody` requests 2.5 KB and performs 42 dups per step (record updates and `List.set` copies through `set_at_b`, which has no owned path on WASM because the LIR does not recompute §17.149's facts until N0b); `sieve` 49× and `spectral_norm` 46× are §17.152's carries; `rbtree`, `binary_trees` and `deltablue` (§17.201 (2)) likewise. The Wasmtime cells are 1.3–2× their Node cells, which is the engine. Decision: (1) **WS-W1, bound performs and clause twins on WASM** (Opus; `sakura_lir/src/{lower,direct}.rs`, `sakura_codegen_wasm/src/func.rs`, `runtime/rt/src/exec.rs`, `ABI.md` §5; one commit, after plan §3.8 row 16's second commit, which edits the same files). A `Perform` that §17.113 (a) binds to a handler literal in the same def, whose clause is direct, lowers to a `Term::Call` of the clause with its activation as an argument; `regions` admits `HandlePush`/`HandlePop` and `StateGet`/`StateSet` on the function's own activations (errata L11 already carries scalar state unboxed), so the clause and the body get twins. The bound call keeps its two duties through one runtime call before the twin (`rt_bound(effect, op)`: the cancellation test and the §17.80 trace record when tracing is on, which is what `rt_perform` does before it dispatches), so traces are equal with and without twins. A perform under a lambda, under an inner `with` of the same effect, or of a clause that may `fail` or has no twin stays `Term::Perform`, as on JS. Acceptance: `handled_d1`, `_d4` and `_d16` at n = 1,000 run at most 10 frames (from 1,005) and `handled_suspend` at most 10 more than its suspensions; `handled_fwd` keeps its 2,005 until WS-P10 lands on both backends; every `tests/semantics` program and trace identical under `--no-direct`; `direct_region_restart` extended to a clause twin aborting after a state write (the frame path then repeats the write from the state the abort left, so a fixture pins the state's value after an abort); `bench_counters.txt` reviewed. Advisory direction: `handled_d1` under 10× hand JS on Node. (2) **WS-W2, lambda twins and native callbacks** (Opus; the same files plus `runtime/rt/src/{list,builtins}.rs`, `sakura_codegen_wasm/src/link.rs`; one commit after (1)). Every direct lambda and clause gets a twin in the closure convention, and a twin-index table beside the code table maps a closure's code to it (0 for none); a `Callee::Closure` in a region calls the twin through `call_indirect` when the table has one and `rt_call_value` otherwise; the callback-taking builtins (`List.map`, `fold`, `filter`, `all`, `any`, `find`, `sort_by`, `Map.fold` and the rest as `builtins.sigs` lists them) take a native path when the callback's closure has a twin, looping on the twin through a linker-resolved `dispatch_twin` as `run` dispatches frames today, and the frame path otherwise; the gauge charges each twin entry as §5 states, and a native loop aborting past the gauge drops what it built and the caller repeats the call through frames (plan §7 L4 item 3's rule, observable only as `restarts`). Generic callback arguments stay boxed words (ABI §4; the unboxed-leaf question is §17.177's). Acceptance: `contracts_plain`, `pipeline`, `shapes`, `map_set` and `nbody` at their smallest size run at most 10 frames each (from 1,121, 2,508, 5,010, 2,005 and 7,005), and `contracts` within 10 of `contracts_plain` or its reason recorded; dups and drops unchanged on every program; `audit_owned` and the differential run clean; `--no-direct` and trace parity; `direct_region_restart` gains a native `List.map` aborting at element two with the partial list freed (`live` back to its value before the call). (3) **WS-W1 and WS-W2 are M4 exit items.** The exit gate's (e) reads the §17.109 envelope after they land, not before: `handled` against Koka C (3.0), the allocation kernels against OCaml and Koka (2.5) and `nbody` against Rust (3.0) are each dominated by frames that plan §7 L4 item 2 assigned to M4-OPT and §17.199 closed M4-OPT without; a 30× miss is not a carry. The §17.118 bars stay met (§17.199 (3)). (4) **WS-W3, the frame protocol's own cost, is measured now and fixed in M5.** Astra measures all-frame `fib` 30 under Wasmtime with `perf`, attributing `frame_alloc`, `arena_new`, `after`, `drop` and the dispatch; Opus fixes what is attributed (a per-size free list or a cached arena slot are the expected shapes); acceptance: all-frame `fib` 2× faster, twins unchanged. It gates nothing: a frame is the slow path by design (measured; ruling in §17.218: attribution before a fix). (5) **The allocation-bound set is carried to M5 by name**: `nbody`, `sieve`, `spectral_norm`, `rbtree`, `binary_trees` and `deltablue` on WASM wait for N0b (the owned facts on the LIR, so `List.set` takes its owned path), N3 and N4, and are read at §17.177's windows with their JS carries; `bench/deltas.md` records each with this cause (Astra). (6) **The Wasmtime-over-Node factor is recorded, not pursued**; the envelope is read on both engines as §17.109 says. Why: the counters name the frames exactly; the mechanism (twins) has existed since L4 and the two rows extend it to the two shapes the benchmarks are made of; speed by default is the owner's goal and M4 is WASM's milestone. Rejected: a handler-free subset or a `@direct` annotation (§17.117); reading the M4 envelope now with the misses carried (the carries would be M4-OPT's own unbuilt half); unboxing generic callbacks in WS-W2 (an ABI change, §17.177's question); moving `List.fold`'s loop into emitted code (one loop per builtin per program, where the runtime loop with a native call is one function per builtin). Performance: this entry is the measured item; the advisory ratios are directions and the envelope is read at the window. Changed: §14.3 (M4 acceptance pointer), plan `m3-completion-and-m4.md` §3.8 (rows 21 and 22, the exit gate), §7 L4 item 2 (pointer), plan `performance-r1.md` §9 (rows WS-W1–WS-W3, the carry row, order after round 21), plan `m5-native.md` §3 (one row). Impact: Opus (WS-W1 then WS-W2, after row 16), Astra (WS-W3's measurement, the carry rows), the owner (the window after WS-W2).

212. **A finished root task is released by the host: `rt_task_free` after the outcome is decoded (WS-B finding (b); ABI 1.2.2; round 21).** Facts: `sakura_wasm_host.js`'s `runTask` returns `this.outcome(task, kind)` and never calls `rt_task_free`, which `wasm.rs` exports and the WASI command calls; `exec::task_free` releases the outcome value or defect text, the task's five stacks, its arena slot and the task object, which are the four objects and 344 bytes `live_objects` grows by per `start(-1)`; the harness's Node cells call `main` once per sample on one instance, so their RSS column grows with the sample count. Decision: Opus, small, now (`runtime/js/sakura_wasm_host.js` and its exports wrapper, `runtime/js/test/wasm_host.test.js`, `runtime/rt/ABI.md` §6.2). `runTask` frees the task on every return after `run` reported an outcome (`ok`, `fail`, `defect`, `exit`, `cancelled`), after `outcome` has decoded the payload; the `every task is waiting` return leaves its task, which is not `DONE`, and an instance is not reused after that `D090`. ABI §6.2 gains "the host releases a root task with `rt_task_free(task)` once it has read its outcome; the id is invalid after" (1.2.2, PATCH, wording). Test: on one instance, `main` of a returning program and of a failing one run twice each, with `live_objects` and `live` equal after the first and the second run; `tests/semantics` on WASM unchanged. Memory safety: `task_free` asserts `DONE` and a freed id traps (a host bug, ABI §8.6); the runtime's own `task_free` test (`runtime/rt/tests/exec.rs`) runs on the host under the §17.173 lanes as today, and the counter test above is the WASM-side check, since Miri and Valgrind do not see the guest heap. WS-B re-reads the RSS column at the next advisory run; no result changes. Why: the export exists, the WASI path uses it, and the host forgot it. Rejected: freeing inside `run` when the root completes (the host reads the outcome after `run` returns); releasing on the next `start` (hides the order). Changed: plan `m3-completion-and-m4.md` §3.8 (row 23). Impact: Opus; an ABI PATCH. **Landed as `92f4158e`: the export had to be added to the linked module; §17.222 states the optional-export rule under which it stays a PATCH.**

213. **The lane's four questions: `json_validate`'s Wasmtime cell waits for N1b's JSON, `bench envelope` combines the two suites, the JS nightly becomes a matrix with the native panel run once, and `tail_clique`'s ports go to Sol with `verify.py` reporting missing ports instead of stopping (WS-B `7f409eeb`, `daa50f1c`; round 21).** Decision: (1) **`json_validate` is a Node-host cell at M4.** `Json.decode` and `Json.encode` are host imports; the Node host has them in `sakura_runtime.js` and the WASI embedding answers `D090`. §17.109's "WASM under Wasmtime and Node" reads, for a program whose imports the WASI embedding lacks, as its Node cell, recorded `meta.absent` on Wasmtime, neither a miss nor a carry; the `tokenizer`/`json_validate` envelope against Rust (3.0) is read on Node for `json_validate`. The Rust JSON codec lands with N1b (plan `m5-native.md` §2, whose services list already names JSON): written once in `runtime/rt` (schema-driven over runtime values, `no_std`, as `show.rs` is), so the WASI command and the native host share it, with `nested_options_json.sak` and the `std/json` fixtures passing on all three; the Node host keeps its JS codec until that corpus passes on the Rust one, when switching is decided. Rejected: a WASI JSON now (M4 has no consumer for it but one bench cell); a Wasmtime cell timing a build that stubs JSON (not the program). (2) **`bench envelope <wasm.json> <native.json> [--json out] [--md out]`** (Opus WS-B, small, before the window; `bench/harness/bench.py`, `test_bench.py`, `bench/README.md`): refuses two files whose `meta.commit` or `meta.host.id` differ; prints `advisory (host load L)` as `compare` does when either is not quiet; pairs program × size × engine (Node host, Wasmtime) against the §17.109 lanes, the numeric kernels against Rust matched (3.0) and Go (2.0) with `tail_clique` reported beside `countdown`, the allocation kernels against OCaml (2.5) and both Koka lanes (2.5), `handled_*` against Koka (3.0), `tokenizer` and `json_validate` against Rust matched (3.0); one row per cell with its ceiling and met or miss; exit 0 (a report; the gate is the owner's reading). `bless` is unchanged. Astra runs it at the window; its markdown is the M4 gate report's envelope section and goes to `bench/deltas.md`. (3) **The JS nightly is trimmed by shape, not by sizes** (Sol; `.github/workflows/bench.yml`). The `nightly` job ran `--suite all` four times in one 180-minute runner, building and timing the native panel once per JS engine, which does not depend on the engine. It becomes a matrix of `lane × engine` running `--suite js` (four jobs, `timeout-minutes: 120` each, the artefact names kept) and one `nightly-native` job running `--suite native` for both lanes (its own timeout; the Go, OCaml and Koka toolchains installed only there), with `hyperfine` cached by version (`actions/cache` on its binary); `--sizes all --stable` stay, since the nightly is the trend evidence on the largest sizes. If a `js` cell still exceeds its timeout, the `idiomatic` lane moves to the weekly schedule beside `noise`; nothing else is cut. `nightly-wasm` is read from its first scheduled run against its 150 minutes. Acceptance: the scheduled run finishes with six artefacts, each job under its timeout. (4) **`tail_clique`'s ports** (Sol, by the WS-S protocol; `bench/programs/tail_clique/{rust,go,python,typescript}/` over the `_shared` kernels as the others): the state machine as a loop with the control's checked `Int` operations, checksums from `meta.toml`; in the same commit `verify.py` records a missing port as `skip: missing port <program>/<language>` and fails at the end listing them, so one new program never stops the checker. `tail_clique`'s envelope cells are reported with `countdown`'s family (Rust matched 3.0, Go 2.0 at M4) and are not gating at M4 exit, which keeps §17.109's fifteen; M5 reads it with the family. Why: each is the smallest change that gives the window what it needs. Changed: plan `performance-r1.md` §9 (three rows), plan `m3-completion-and-m4.md` §3.8 (row 24), plan `m5-native.md` §2 N1b (the JSON placement). Impact: Opus WS-B (2), Sol (3), (4), Astra (the window), N1b's owner (1).

214. **Compile latency after WS-C1b and WS-C1c: both ratified as built (the fixpoint over per-file call facts and the cut carried run), the std-first flip lands with WS-C2, sharing instead of copying is a row after WS-C2, and WS-C2's acceptance is restated with `makers` (`3c39605f`..`87c65957`; `research/10-evidence/compile-latency-r1.md`; round 21).** Facts (indicative, loaded host): an analysis that reuses std costs 1.09 ms on the phase lines (8.28 before; target ≤ 3 ms, met), the carried phases 0.01–0.11 ms; wall per `Analysis::analyze` 10.58 → 2.13 ms, WS-C1c removing the evicted entry's `Teardown` walk of std's 46 trees; a single `sakura check` is unchanged at 25.4 ms, as §17.204 said it would be until WS-C2. Of the 1.09 ms, the profile puts most in copies of std's results made per analysis: the predecessor interner's tables and typed bodies in `check_program_reusing`, std's HIR defs and bodies in lower, std's symbols in `symbols::index`. The std-first flip (§17.204 (1a)) measured std-first 1.04–1.11 ms dearer than fresh-first (paired medians, p25 0.95) against an acceptance of 1 ms. Decision: (1) **The flip waits for WS-C2 and lands as its last commit.** Its acceptance compared two in-process paths, and the miss is the reuse path's own 1 ms, inside the probe's spread; its purpose was one path for `check`, the LSP and the MCP server, and in a fresh process that path exists only once WS-C2 supplies std from the cache (before it, a one-off `check` std-first is §17.204 (1a)'s 41 against 23 ms). The 1 ms in-process comparison is withdrawn; the flip's acceptance becomes WS-C2's (`check_hello` ≤ 10 ms with the cache), plus: an LSP process's first analysis is std-first, the determinism test keeps its fully fresh side through `analyze(.., [])`, and `std_reuse_matches_a_fresh_analysis` holds for the first analysis too. (2) **WS-C1f: share, do not copy** (Opus; `sakura_types` (the `Interner` in two layers: a frozen std layer behind an `Arc` with stable `TypeId`s and a per-analysis extension; `TypedProgram`'s std bodies and schemes as `Arc` slices), `sakura_hir` (`Program`'s carried modules as `Arc<Module>`), `sakura_analysis` (a carried module's `Symbol`s as an `Arc` slice); after WS-C2 and WS-C1d, before WS-C1e). Yes, as a row, and after WS-C2: WS-C2 serialises exactly these types, and serde's `rc` feature serialises an `Arc<T>` as `T`, so landing the cache first costs WS-C1f nothing while landing WS-C1f first would move WS-C2's shapes under it; and no target waits on it (the cache's reuse budget of 1.5 ms is met at 1.09; the row buys up to about 0.9 ms per in-process analysis, which is the LSP's per-edit floor). Acceptance: a reusing analysis ≤ 0.4 ms on the phase lines with `check` ≤ 0.15 and lower ≤ 0.1; `--dump` outputs, `sakura doc --search` and the LSP's symbols byte-identical fresh and reused; §17.194's order test green; no `TypeId` of the std layer changes between two analyses in one process (a test interns a user type after std and reads a std type's id before and after). (3) **Ratified as built: per-file call facts with a fixpoint, and the cut carried run.** §17.204 (2)'s "union of the carried sets and the changed files' fresh ones" was wrong by the finding that a call chain crosses files: each file keeps what it adds (its `where` clauses' callees and, per function, its body's and contracts' callees) and the evaluated set is the least fixpoint over every file's facts, at 0.05 ms. The carried run of reused modules ends before the first module importing one past it (`sakura_hir::carried`), which keeps the import-cycle `V1006` the review found and leaves std's run whole; §17.204 (1b)'s predicate reads with it. §17.204 (2) reads as built. (4) **`makers` is part of the cached `Program`.** WS-C2 serialises `sakura_hir::Module` whole, every field, `makers` (the `V1006` opaque-type hint computed at collection) included; its acceptance gains: the `V1006` fixtures and the import-cycle fixture produce byte-identical diagnostics against a cached std and a fresh one. A field added to `Module` or `TypedProgram` later is cached by the derive, and the build-keyed cache name (§17.195 (3)) invalidates the file. (5) **WS-C2 is next for the latency owner**, with this acceptance and no other: the file holds std's `Program`, `TypedProgram` (interner, schemes, typed bodies), `Symbol`s with `file` and `def`, the per-file interface hashes and evaluated-name facts of (3), the snapshot's file hashes and no syntax tree (`parses` slots empty, as WS-C1c leaves them), keyed by the compiler build under `$SAKURA_CACHE_DIR`, off with `SAKURA_NO_CACHE=1`; a fresh process reads, verifies, deserialises, runs the reuse path over the user files and analyses the user modules; outputs byte-identical with and without the cache on the UI corpus, the examples and every `--dump` (the fixtures of (4) included); on the hello-world phase lines, recorded in the research note before the window: deserialise ≤ 2 ms (over it, the note says where and a zero-copy format is decided then), reuse path ≤ 1.5 ms, the hello module ≤ 1 ms, outside `analyze` ≤ 4 ms; `bench compile` gains `check_hello`, `test_hello` and `target_ms`; targets `check_hello` ≤ 10 ms and `check_cold_10k` ≤ 45 ms p95 at the quiet window; the flip of (1) as its last commit; STABILITY.md names the cache a per-build artefact with no compatibility promise. Why: the number says the walk is gone and the copies are what is left; the cache is the row that moves the only unmet target; sharing is worth a row but not a reorder. Rejected: the flip now on a 0.1 ms reading (the acceptance measured the wrong thing); WS-C1f before WS-C2 (moves the cache's types under it); a zero-copy format decided now (§17.204 (4)). Performance: WS-C2 is the measured item; numbers enter `bench/deltas.md` from the window. Changed: §17.204 (1a), (2) (pointers), plan `performance-r1.md` §9 (row WS-C1f, WS-C2's acceptance, order after round 21). Impact: Opus (WS-C2, then WS-C1d, WS-C1f, WS-C1e), Astra (the window), the owner none.

215. **Order after round 21 (`git log origin/main` at `87c65957`; round 21).** State: since §17.210, landed on `main`: the WASM bench lane and `tail_clique` (`543571d1`..`daa50f1c`), WS-C1b and WS-C1c with their note (`3c39605f`..`87c65957`). In flight, as far as this round can see: §17.210's host fixes (rows 1 and 2) and the suppression rule (3). New this round, ranked, each one commit unless stated: **1** §17.212 the task release (Opus, small; `runtime/js/sakura_wasm_host.js`, its test, `ABI.md`; after §17.210's rows 1 and 2, same host file); **2** plan §3.8 row 16, commit 1 then commit 2 (Opus; §17.203's lead, still the first M4 exit item, and the LIR and WASM files WS-W1 waits on); **3** §17.211 (1) WS-W1 (Opus; after 2's second commit); **4** §17.211 (2) WS-W2 (Opus; after 3); **5** WS-C2 as §17.214 (5) states it (Opus, the latency owner; `sakura_analysis`, the serde derives in `sakura_hir` and `sakura_types`, workspace `Cargo.toml`, `bench/harness/bench.py`, STABILITY.md; now, colliding with nothing above), then WS-C1d, WS-C1f (§17.214 (2)) and WS-C1e in that order under the same owner; **6** §17.213 (2) `bench envelope` (Opus WS-B, small; now); **7** §17.213 (3) the nightly matrix and (4) the `tail_clique` ports with `verify.py` (Sol; now); **8** §17.211 (4) WS-W3's measurement and (5)'s carry rows in `bench/deltas.md` (Astra; now, no code); **9** the WASM envelope window (the owner, once 4 has landed and the machine is idle; Astra runs `--suite wasm` and `--suite native` then `bench envelope`); **10** §17.213 (1)'s JSON codec with N1b (its owner, M5). §17.210's list is otherwise unchanged: §17.207 (3), §17.209 (4), WS-P9b-lit after row 16's first commit, N1a, WS-P13a. Collisions: 1 follows §17.210's rows 1 and 2 in the host file; 2, 3 and 4 are one sequence on `sakura_lir/src/lower.rs` and `sakura_codegen_wasm/src/func.rs` under one owner; 5's sequence is `sakura_analysis` and the two crates' derives, which nothing else edits; 6, 7 and 8 are `bench/**` and `.github/**`. Why: the leak is a one-line correctness fix in a file already being edited; the WASM rows are M4's exit path and share files with row 16, so they queue behind it; the latency sequence is independent and WS-C2 is the only row that moves a missed target. Rejected: starting WS-W1 before row 16's second commit (the same files); holding WS-C2 for WS-C1f (§17.214 (2)); reading the envelope before WS-W2 (§17.211 (3)). Performance: WS-W1, WS-W2 and WS-C2 are the measured items. Changed: plans `performance-r1.md` §9 (order after round 21), `m3-completion-and-m4.md` §3.8 (state line, rows 21–24, the exit gate). Impact: Opus, Sol, Astra as named; the owner (the window).

216. **HTTP limits as landed: the WASM rejection handler answers a defect because every network failure is already `Err` inside the operations, the byte-count rule gains its upper bound, the budget is read per request on JS and per instance on WASM, and the pre-existing serve crash on a request over the copy budget is fixed under §17.159 (6) as a `413`, not a `500` (`cfe90b87`, `187a8f80`, `cffce484`; §17.209; round 21).** Facts: `bytesEnv` refuses everything but decimal digits from 1 to 2^53 − 1 (a longer digit string is `Infinity` through `Number`, which would be unlimited, and anything past 2^53 is rounded); JS `http_send` reads it per request before the fetch leaves and the WASM host reads it once per instance (`copyBudget ??=`) at its first copy or when `serve_listen` starts, so an invalid value is the run's `D091` at the first host answer on WASM, HTTP or not, and only in `send` on JS; `http_send` catches a failure of the body read and answers `Err(reason)`, never a truncated `Ok`; the WASM `async` import settles every request, dropping a cancelled one and answering any other rejection with its defect (`hostFailure`'s `D091`, `D090` for a `HostFault`), which departs from §17.209 (2)'s "`Err` for a `Result` schema". Both review models argued the departure: the `http_*` builtins answer every network failure, a cut body included, as `Err` inside their own chains, so only a host bug reaches the handler, and a host bug labelled a retriable `Err` on WASM but `D091` on JS would split the backends. Decision: (1) **Ratified: the rejection handler is the last resort and answers a defect on both backends.** The invariant it rests on is stated as the rule: every failure a program can retry (a refused connection, a failed fetch, a cut body, a stream read error, a cancelled request) is answered `Err` by the operation that owns the request, before any handler of last resort sees it; the handler sees only what the operation did not foresee, which is a host bug, a defect by ABI §8.6. §17.209 (2)'s "`Err(failureOf(e))` for a `Result` schema" is withdrawn; its acceptance (the cut body `Err` on both backends through the CLI, the cancel mid-body with no unhandled rejection, the host test whose operations reject) is the test of the invariant and stays. (2) **Ratified: the upper bound.** §17.209 (1)'s rule reads "a string of decimal digits with value from 1 to 2^53 − 1"; the breaking forms (`1e3`, `" 42"`, `0x400`) are named in the CHANGELOG with the migration, as built. (3) **Ratified: no memoisation in `bytesEnv`.** The WASM host memoises per instance and JS reads per request; a Sakura program cannot change its process's environment (`Env` is read-only, §10.2), so the two are unobservable from a program, and the one visible difference is documented as built: with an invalid value a WASM program fails at its first host answer even without HTTP, a JS program only in `send`. Rejected: memoising in the runtime (a process-wide cell for a value two hosts already hold where they need it). (4) **The serve crash is a §17.159 (6) item, fixed now; the request case answers `413`.** With a copy budget below an incoming request's size (the request within the 1 MiB cap of §17.159 (6)), the WASM host's serve callback throws the copy defect while copying the request in, and Node ends the process. §17.159 (6)'s rules already decide both halves: a request the server cannot accept because of a size limit is `413` with the connection closed and the handler not run, as the 1 MiB cap is today (the copy budget is one more such limit, set by the operator rather than the host); the handler's own reply exceeding the budget is the server's fault, `D091` on stderr and `500`, the server running on. So: not `500` for the request case; `413` there, `500` for the reply case, the defect reported once on stderr in both, and the server survives both. Opus, small, now, in `sakura_wasm_host.js` after §17.212's commit in the same file (`runtime/js/test/wasm_host.test.js`, `runtime/rt/ABI.md` §8.5 one sentence: incoming requests count against the per-instance budget and a request over it is refused with `413` before the guest sees it). Acceptance: `SAKURA_MAX_COPY_BYTES=1024`, a 2 KiB request is `413` and the next request is served; a reply over the budget is `500` with the defect on stderr and the next request is served; the JS host, which copies nothing into a guest, is unchanged and the two-backend serve fixtures agree on every other status. Why: the operations own their failures, so the handler's answer is a classification of bugs, not of network weather; a budget that `Number` cannot hold is not a budget; the serve rules of §17.159 (6) already say what a refused request and a broken reply answer. Rejected: `500` for a request the server refused on size (tells the client the server broke when its request was too large for this deployment); reading the budget on JS once at load (a bad value would crash a program that never sends). Changed: §17.209 (pointers), plan `m3-completion-and-m4.md` §3.8 (row 20 done, row 25). Impact: Opus (row 25, small); the owner (the review of `187a8f80` requested in its message stands, now with (1)'s rule as its reading). **(4) landed as `ea22b159`; the older served-request findings its review raised are §17.223.**

217. **Dense `Int`-keyed state: `List` is the mechanism, no dense `Map` and no new type; WS-P13c measures `deltablue` on `List` before anything is built; §17.201's 25% is a time reduction; the flat representation stays §17.177 (1)'s window, with `deltablue`-on-`List` as its program (WS-P13a `b5a35240`, `research/10-evidence/ws-p13a/README.md`; §17.201 (3); round 22).** Facts (advisory, loaded host, §17.153): Node n = 5000 baseline 257.8 ms; owned inserts alone 199.4 ms (22.7% less time, 1.29×; confirmation 22.9%); owned inserts plus `vars`/`cons` as flat JS arrays 29.3 ms (88.6% less, 7.85× hand JS); the profiles put `hget` at 32–42%, `setAt` 17.5% before ownership, and the flat arm's residue in GC, records and planner work. The port allocates every id as `Map.len(p.vars)` at `new_var` and never removes one, so its keys are `0..n−1` in insertion order: a sequence, held in a hash trie with an insertion log. `List` already is that sequence: `push` (the id is `len`), `set` (one path copy, O(log32 n), in place when owned, §17.126, §17.149), `get` and `xs[i]` through the leaf cache (§17.116), `len`; the flat arm's `$denseGet` is `list_get_expect` on a JS array (§17.151). WS-P11b's per-map key kind measured +2–5% and was not kept (§17.201 (1)); §17.152 (1) and §17.177 (1) hold every representation change to the windows; `sieve` was re-ported to `List.set` when std gained it (§17.126); AWFY's DeltaBlue keeps variables and constraints in arrays and objects. Decision: (1) **The language mechanism is `List<A>`.** One rule, written once in `std/INDEX.md` and once in the skill's stdlib reference: *an `Int` key you allocate as `len(xs)` and never remove indexes a `List`; `Map<Int, V>` is for sparse, removable or externally chosen keys.* No `Array` or `Vector` type (§17.117 and §17.126 stand: value semantics, one sequence type, `W8001` names a forced copy); no dense representation inside `Map` (a second representation in two runtimes, a branch on every `Map` operation that WS-P11b measured for a smaller field, and a representation transition on `remove` of a non-last key or `insert` past `len` that no program can see and no agent can predict); no compiler rewrite of `Map<Int, V>` to `List<V>` (the type is the program's statement).
